\displaystyle\quad-D_{j}D^{i}N-N\mathrm{\Lambda}\mathchoice{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}\,,

(2.53)

together with the energy and momentum constraints

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{K}^{2}-\mathcal{K}_{ij}\,\mathcal{K}^{ij}

\displaystyle=16\pi GE+2\mathrm{\Lambda}\,,

(2.54)

\displaystyle D_{k}\mathchoice{\mathcal{K}^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}-D_{i}\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=8\pi GJ_{i}\,.

(2.55)

Here,
D_{i}
is the induced covariant derivative associated with the metric
\bm{h}
, hence we introduced
D^{i}:=h^{ij}D_{j}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{ij}
are the components of the
3
-Ricci tensor
\bm{\mathcal{R}ic}
associated to it, with
\mathcal{R}=\tr_{\bm{h}}\bm{\mathcal{R}ic}
.

Another form of (
2.51
) or (
2.53
) turns out to be useful. We introduce the trace-free part of the second fundamental form, i.e.,

\mathcal{A}_{ij}:=\mathcal{K}_{\langle ij\rangle}=\mathcal{K}_{ij}-\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{K}\,h_{ij}\,,

(2.56)

and write down the evolution equation for the mean curvature
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
.
First, taking the trace of (
2.53
) and using the energy constraint (
2.54
), we find the evolution equation for the mean curvature
\mathcal{K}
(cf., e.g.,
Alcubierre 2008
;
Baumgarte and Shapiro 2010
;
Rendall 2008
;
Smarr and York 1978
):

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\mathcal{L}_{\bm{N}})\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=N\left[\mathcal{K}_{ij}\,\mathcal{K}^{ij}+4\pi G\left(E+3P\right)-\mathrm{\Lambda}\right]

(2.57)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\bm{h}}N\,,

And for
\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{A}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{A}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{A}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}
, we find (cf., e.g.,
(
Vigneron and Barzegar 2025
, Equation (4.13))
)

II.5
Principal scalar invariants of the second fundamental form

We add a useful rewriting of the scalar parts of the Einstein equations in the following which is particularly useful for analyzing the current warp-drive spacetimes.

Using the principal scalar invariants for
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
, from (
2.57
) we obtain

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\mathcal{L}_{\bm{N}}){\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=N\left\{{\rm I}^{2}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]-2{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]+4\pi G\left(E+3P\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\Delta_{\bm{h}}N-N\mathrm{\Lambda}\,,

(2.59)

which is the Raychaudhuri equation for the case when
\bm{n}=\bm{u}
and
\bm{\omega}=\bm{0}
. Using (
2.51
) we find

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\mathcal{L}_{\bm{N}}){\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=8\pi GNE\,{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

(2.60)

\displaystyle-N\left(\mathchoice{\mathcal{R}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{R}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{R}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{R}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}-8\pi II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}

\displaystyle-{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]\Delta_{\bm{h}}N+2\mathchoice{{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}D_{j}D^{i}N\,,

where
{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]_{ij}
is defined as in
Section

I.1
, and we used the energy constraint (
2.54
) in the new form

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}=8\pi GE+\mathrm{\Lambda}-{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]\,.

(2.62)

Remark II.5
.

The system of evolution equations (
2.59
)–(
2.61
), has been derived before for the case where
N=1
,
N^{i}=0
,
\mathchoice{S^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{S^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{S^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{S^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}=0
, and
\mathchoice{\Theta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\Theta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\Theta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\Theta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}=-\mathchoice{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}
in
(
Brunswic and Buchert 2020
, Equations (5)-(7))
. Of course, it is equivalent to the usual (3+1)-decomposition of the Einstein equations. However, there are some advantages in this form compared to the usual form for our study of warp-drive spacetimes (cf. (
3.33
) and
III.9
below). (These equations are valid in three dimensions; one can easily generalize them to the
(n+1)
-dimensional case, as it is done in
Brunswic and Buchert 2020
for the special case studied there.)

III
Classification of warp-drive spacetimes

In this section, we give a clear classification of existing warp-drive proposals and their generalizations. But, before the classification, we should make precise what we mean by a warp bubble. First, we assume that the topology of the spacetime is trivial, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}
, as it is always assumed in the warp-drive literature.
10
10

10

This constitutes the main difference from the alternative proposal for FTL travels, i.e., traversable wormholes, where FTL travels are achieved via nontrivial topology.
And then, in line with
Natário 2002
, we define warp bubble as follows.

Definition III.1

(Warp bubble)
.

A
warp bubble
is said to be generated by a vector field
\bm{W}\in\mathfrak{X}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
if, for a given foliation
\Sigma_{t}
with the induced metric
\bm{h}
and for a constant
R>0
, the following holds

(
i
)

\bm{W}=\bm{0}
for any point
P_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
at a small spatial distance from a point
P_{s}(t)\in\Sigma_{t}
, called the
center of the bubble
, i.e.,
{\rm d}_{\bm{h}}(P_{s},P_{i})\ll R
. This region represents the interior of the bubble.

(
ii
)

\bm{W}=-\bm{V}_{s}(t)
for any point
P_{e}\in\mathcal{M}
far away from the center, i.e.,
{\rm d}_{\bm{h}}(P_{s},P_{e})\gg R
, where
\bm{D}\bm{V}_{s}=0
. This region represents the exterior of the bubble.

We say that the warp bubble has a characteristic radius
R
and a velocity
\bm{V}_{s}(t)\in\mathfrak{X}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
.

We call a spacetime that admits a warp bubble by
III.1
a
warp-drive
spacetime.
This definition is very generic. In particular, it does not say,
a priori
, how the vector
\bm{W}
is related to the metric or the matter content. This relation will depend on the precise type of warp-drive model which will be discussed in
Section

III.1
.
However, despite being very general,
III.1
is the core idea of such spacetimes that distinguishes them from other spacetimes, especially the black hole and cosmological ones. We will give a more concrete definition for the warp bubble in
Section

III.6
for the special case of
R
-Warp models.

Remark III.2
.

The condition
\bm{D}\bm{V}_{s}=0
is the manifestly covariant formulation of spatially constant vector field. In particular, if the spatial metric is flat, this condition ensures that in Cartesian coordinates (where
h_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
, see blow) the components of
\bm{V}_{s}
are spatial constants.

Now, we are in a position to give a classification of the existing (consistent) warp-drive spacetimes.
For this we give a list of restrictions that comply with the assumptions underlying them. We recognize four main restrictions that cover almost all warp-drive proposals, which are either spatially conformally flat, or spatially flat. The latter is occasionally referred to as Natário warp drive that is dubbed “generic warp drives” by
Santiago et al. 2022
, covering most if not all proposals in the literature.
We shall not use the word “generic” here, and we rather use “restricted warp drives” (
R
-Warp
for short) introduced first in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
through the following list (see
III.3
below).

III.1
Restrictions imposed on current warp-drive spacetimes

Based on the
3+1
-formalism introduced in
Section

II
, we list the restrictions imposed in the current literature on warp-drive spacetimes.

R1

Flow-orthogonality:

Assuming a
flow-orthogonal
foliation of spacetime, i.e., where the
4
-velocity
\bm{u}
is aligned with the normal to the hypersurfaces of a spacetime
3+1
foliation (
\bm{u}=\bm{n}
). As a consequence, the covariant vorticity field has to vanish.

R2

Fixing lapse and shift:

Choosing
a priori
diffeomorphism degrees of freedom in terms of specific choices of lapse and shift functions. The mostly used assumption is a constant lapse function to avoid the time dilatation of two distant Eulerian observers. Then, the (fluid) covariant acceleration
\bm{a}
has to vanish as a consequence of
R1
, hence we work in a
geodesic slicing
of spacetime (cf., e.g.,
(
Gourgoulhon 2012
, Section 10.2.1)
). Additionally, some restrictions on the shift can be considered, e.g., by taking
\bm{N}=-\bm{W}
, i.e., the shift generates the warp bubble.

R3

Special curvature of spatial slices:
Assuming either spatially conformally flat or flat slices:

R3a

Spatial conformal flatness:
Assuming that the spatial slices are conformally flat.

R3b

Spatial flatness:
Assuming that the spatial Ricci curvature
\bm{\mathcal{R}}
vanishes.

R4

Asymptotic flatness:

Assuming that the spacetime is asymptotically flat.

In addition to the restrictions listed above, we specify the following restrictions which may appear in warp-drive proposals:

R5

Kinematical restrictions:

Restrictions on kinematic variables like the rate of expansion, the shear tensor and the (coordinate) vorticity tensor.

R6

Cosmological constant:

Assuming that the cosmological constant
\mathrm{\Lambda}
vanishes.

Imposing different subsets of these restrictions on a warp-drive spacetime will lead to different spacetimes.
Previously, we distinguished two general classes of possible warp-drive spacetimes in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
, i.e., the
R
-Warp and
T
-Warp models. Here, we narrow down the definition of
R
-Warp models given in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
, based on the list above.

Definition III.3

(
R
-Warp model)
.

A warp-drive spacetime that satisfies
R1
+
R2
+
R3R3b
+
R4
(+
R6
) is an
R
-Warp model.

Remark III.4
.

One might wonder why we do not call this class of warp-drive spacetimes the Natário warp drive as it is sometimes done in the literature. The reason is the absence of the important condition (
3.37
) (see
Section

III.7
) in
III.3
.

Remark III.5
.

We included the asymptotic flatness as a restriction to the list, although it is not mentioned explicitly in the main works by, e.g.,
Alcubierre 1994
,
Natário 2002
, and
Broeck 1999
, but it is naturally the case for the Alcubierre warp drive (see
IV.15
), and its generalizations based on the same idea. This, of course, satisfies
R6
automatically. This is consistent with the idea of a warp bubble that is well localized. This latter property together with the asymptotic flatness, i.e., definition of a warp bubble and
R4
, play a fundamental role in the warp-drive spacetimes and their properties, such as the violation of energy conditions (see, e.g.,
Section

III.1
). However, a precise definition for
R4
is needed, which we will give in
IV.13
, to avoid possible errors.

R
-Warp models include many existing proposals, whereas there is no proposed model of type
T
-Warp, except from the one introduced in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
. This class of warp-drive spacetime essentially violates
R1
, hence it is tilted. They might potentially violate other restrictions as well, but the tilt is their main character.
Here, we distinguish yet another class which lies in a sense in between of the two classes mentioned above, by dropping two restrictions from
R
-Warp, i.e.,
R2
and
R3
, keeping only
R1
. The reason for this is that the current warp-drive spacetimes are based on the idea that the motion of the warp bubble is associated with the shift vector and not the tilt, hence allowing for keeping
R1
even when dropping
R2
and
R3
.
We call this new class of models
S
-Warp
(see
Section

III.2
below). In a sense, one has the hierarchy
R
-Warp
\subset

S
-Warp
\subset

T
-Warp. Hence, in order to classify existing proposals in the literature, we commence by considering
S
-Warp models as their broadest possible form with generic spatial metric, lapse function and shift vector, and we then recover other proposals by applying
R1
to
R5
consecutively.

Remark III.6
.

The classification done by
Bobrick and Martire 2021
is at best vague, suffers from some errors (see, e.g.,
11
), and does not satisfy
I.1
.

Since most known warp-drive spacetimes do not include the cosmological constant, we impose
R6
(i.e., setting
\mathrm{\Lambda}=0
) for the spacetimes considered in this paper from now on. As remarked earlier,
R6
is already included in
R
-Warp models.

Throughout we use the (spatial) coordinate system given in (
2.6
), and call coordinate systems
Cartesian
in which
h_{ij}=e^{2\psi}\delta_{ij}
for both
\psi\neq 0
and
\psi=0
cases. We perform all calculations in such coordinates. Moreover, by abuse of notation, we define
\left|\bm{x}\right|_{\bm{\delta}}:=(\delta_{ij}x^{i}x^{j})^{1/2}
.

Before advancing further, we note that since we take an agonistic approach, we analyze the spacetimes classified below purely from mathematical and geometric point of view, and do not,
a priori
, attribute any physical significance to them.

III.2
Flow-orthogonal spacetimes (
S
-Warp models)

The most general metric which can be generalized based on the existing proposals for warp-drive spacetimes is the one which merely assumes
R1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=h_{ij}(t,\bm{x})\,,

(3.1)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=N(t,\bm{x})\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})=-W^{i}(t,\bm{x})\,.

We call such a model, which merely satisfies
R1
, an
S
-Warp to distinguish it from
R
-Warp introduced in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
, i.e., these are models where the warp-bubble generator is the (fluid) coordinate velocity, i.e.,
\bm{V}=\bm{W}
. Since
S
-Warp models encompass a wide range of possible spacetimes, it does not make sense to write down the Einstein equations for them. Instead, we will write them for more restricted models. Note that we do not,
a priori
, impose
R4
in the following, unless for
R
-Warp models, by definition.

Additionally, since the existing proposals for warp-drive spacetimes are all special cases of
S
-Warp models with flat spatial slices, the principal scalar invariants of the extrinsic curvature
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
(introduced in
Section

II.5
) turn out to be very useful, not only because they encode useful information about the second fundamental form due to its special form it takes for these models, but also because they reveal some valuable relations.

Remark III.7
.

To our knowledge, perhaps except from
Lobo and Visser 2004a
;
Santiago et al. 2022
where it is discussed in an indirect way, the relations involving the principal invariants are not discussed in the literature. Although we agree with
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Section III.B)
that these relations are not useful the way they are advertised in
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
, they reveal however some important structure for warp-drive spacetimes. Our intuition for usefulness of these quantities comes mainly from works in relativistic inhomogeneous cosmology (see, e.g.,
Buchert 1994
;
Ehlers and Buchert 1997
;
Buchert et al. 2020
), although they are introduced and used in continuum mechanics, especially elasticity (see, e.g.,
Truesdell and Toupin 1960
;
Marsden and Hughes 2012
).

As mentioned before, the warp-bubble generator for more general warp-drive spacetimes could be different than the coordinate velocity of the matter (see
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
for a proposition). However, from now on, we will assume that the coordinate velocity always is the warp-bubble generator, i.e.,
\bm{V}=\bm{W}
.

III.3
Spatially conformally flat slices with generic shift vector and lapse function

We assume
R1
and
R3R3a
. More precisely, we assume

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=e^{2\psi}\delta_{ij}\,,

(3.2)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=N(t,\bm{x})\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})\,,

where
\psi:\Sigma_{t}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

is a smooth function at each time
t
, and we expressed the metric components in Cartesian coordinates. Therefore, the second fundamental form in this general case can be determined using the relations from
Appendix

A
and (
2.50
) as follows

\mathcal{K}_{ij}=-\frac{1}{N}\partial_{(i}V_{j)}+\Psi_{ij}\,,

(3.3)

with

\Psi_{ij}:=-\frac{1}{N}\left[N\mathcal{L}_{\bm{n}}\psi\,h_{ij}-2\partial_{(i}\psi V_{j)}\right]\,,

(3.4)

where we used (
2.38
).
Hence,

\displaystyle{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}h^{ij}\partial_{i}V_{j}+{\rm I}[{\bm{\Psi}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{-2\psi}}{N}\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}V_{j}+{\rm I}[{\bm{\Psi}}]\,,

(3.5)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\left[\left(h^{ij}\partial_{i}V_{j}\right)^{2}-h^{ij}h^{k\ell}\partial_{(i}V_{k)}\partial_{(j}V_{\ell)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm II}[{\bm{\Psi}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-4\psi}}{2N^{2}}\left[\left(\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}V_{j}\right)^{2}-\delta^{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\partial_{(i}V_{k)}\partial_{(j}V_{\ell)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm II}[{\bm{\Psi}}]\,,

(3.6)

where we introduced the principal invariants of the symmetric tensor
\Psi_{ij}
in a manner similar to those for
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
.
From (
2.62
) using (
A.4
) it follows

\displaystyle 8\pi GE

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-4\psi}}{2N^{2}}\left[\left(\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}V_{j}\right)^{2}-\delta^{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\partial_{(i}V_{k)}\partial_{(j}V_{\ell)}\right]

(3.7)

\displaystyle+{\rm II}[{\bm{\Psi}}]-e^{-2\psi}\left(2\Delta_{{\bm{\delta}}}\psi+\left|\bm{\partial}\psi\right|_{\bm{\delta}}^{2}\right)\,.

We see that it is in general possible to have positive energy density by choosing
\psi
and the shift vector properly. However, this does not imply that such models are free from detriments and do not violate the WEC.

The metric given in this section covers most, if not all warp-drive spacetimes in the literature, although this general metric has not been analyzed so far in this context.
In the next sections we recover all existing warp-drive proposals by imposing more restrictions.

III.4
Spatially flat metric with generic lapse function and shift vector

Assuming
R1
and
R3R3b
(i.e., setting
\psi=0
hence
\bm{\Psi}=\bm{0}
in the previous subsection), (
3.2
) reduces to

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,,

(3.8)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=N(t,\bm{x})\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})\,.

Since
\bm{\Psi}=\bm{0}
, the second fundamental form and its first and second invariants reduce to

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\partial_{(i}V_{j)}\,,

(3.9a)

\displaystyle{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\partial_{i}V^{i}\,,

(3.9b)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\left[\left(\partial_{i}V^{i}\right)^{2}-\partial^{i}V^{j}\partial_{(i}V_{j)}\right]\,,

(3.9c)

where

\partial^{i}:=\delta^{ij}\partial_{j}\,.

(3.10)

From (
2.62
) we find

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi G}{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

(3.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi GN^{2}}\left[\left(\partial_{i}V^{i}\right)^{2}-\partial^{i}V^{j}\partial_{(i}V_{j)}\right]\,,

which can be rewritten as

E=\frac{1}{16\pi GN^{2}}\Big[\partial_{i}\left(V^{i}\partial_{j}V^{j}-V^{j}\partial_{j}V^{i}\right)-\Omega^{ij}\Omega_{ij}\Big]\,,

(3.12)

as it is shown in
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Equation (7.16))
for the
N=1
case and
(
Shoshany and Snodgrass 2024
, Equation (4.4))
for a generic lapse function. Here, we introduced the
coordinate vorticity
11
11

11

We here follow a traditional convention that is motivated by the comma notation for derivatives, i.e.,
\Omega_{ij}:=V_{[i,j]}
.

\Omega_{ij}:=\partial_{[j}V_{i]}=-\partial_{[i}V_{j]}\,,

(3.13)

which, as will be clear later, plays a central role in
R
-Warp models. We also introduce the
coordinate angular velocity vector
as
12
12

12

Sometimes, instead of the angular velocity vector
\Omega^{i}
, which is the dual to the vorticity tensor, the vorticity vector
2\Omega^{i}
is used interchangeably.

\Omega{}^{i}:=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Omega_{jk}\,,

(3.14)

which yields

\Omega_{ij}=-\epsilon_{ijk}\Omega{}^{k}\,.

(3.15)

We further define the squared magnitude of coordinate vorticity, similar to (
2.21
), as

\Omega^{2}:=\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\Omega^{ij}=\Omega{}^{i}\Omega_{i}\,.

(3.16)

III.5
Spatially conformally flat slices with generic shift vector and unit lapse

Assuming
R1
+
R2
+
R3R3a
, (
3.2
) reduces to

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=e^{2\psi}\delta_{ij}\,,

(3.17)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})\,.

We then have

\mathcal{K}_{ij}=-\partial_{(i}V_{j)}+\Psi_{ij}\,,

(3.18)

where

\Psi_{ij}=-\mathcal{L}_{\bm{n}}\psi\,h_{ij}+2\partial_{(i}\psi V_{j)}\,.

(3.19)

Hence,

\displaystyle{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-e^{-2\psi}\partial^{i}V_{i}+{\rm I}[{\bm{\Psi}}]\,,

(3.20)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-4\psi}}{2}\left[\left(\partial^{i}V_{i}\right)^{2}-\delta^{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\partial_{(i}V_{k)}\partial_{(j}V_{\ell)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm II}[{\bm{\Psi}}]\,,

(3.21)

with

\displaystyle{\rm I}[{\bm{\Psi}}]

\displaystyle=-3\partial_{t}\psi-V^{i}\partial_{i}\psi\,,

(3.22)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[{\bm{\Psi}}]

\displaystyle=3(\partial_{t}\psi)^{2}+2V^{i}\partial_{i}\psi\partial_{t}\psi-V^{2}e^{-2\psi}\left|\bm{\partial}\psi\right|_{\bm{\delta}}^{2}\,,

(3.23)

where we used (
2.38
). Then, from (
3.7
) the Eulerian energy density takes the form

\displaystyle 8\pi GE

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-4\psi}}{2}\left[\left(\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}V_{j}\right)^{2}-\delta^{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\partial_{(i}V_{k)}\partial_{(j}V_{\ell)}\right]

(3.24)

\displaystyle+3(\partial_{t}\psi)^{2}+2V^{i}\partial_{i}\psi\partial_{t}\psi

\displaystyle-e^{-2\psi}\left[2\Delta_{{\bm{\delta}}}\psi+(1+V^{2})\left|\bm{\partial}\psi\right|_{\bm{\delta}}^{2}\right]\,.

III.5.1
Van Den Broeck metric with 3-dimensional shift vector

If one adds
R4
to the list above, and assumes special form for the coordinate velocity à la Alcubierre (based on (
3.45
)) we arrive at the Van Den Broeck metric given in
Broeck 1999
(although he considered only a one-dimensional shift vector).

III.6
Restricted warp drive (
R
-Warp)

Now, we assume
R1
+
R2
+
R3R3b
. This class of warp drive is achieved by just setting
N=1
in
Section

III.4
(equivalently, by inserting
\psi=0
, hence
\bm{\Psi}=\bm{0}
, in
Section

III.5
), i.e.,

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,,

(3.25)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})\,,

or, equivalently,

\bm{g}=-{\mathbf{d}}t^{2}+\delta_{ij}\left({\mathbf{d}}x^{i}-V^{i}{\mathbf{d}}t\right)\left({\mathbf{d}}x^{j}-V^{j}{\mathbf{d}}t\right).

(3.26)

Assuming further
R4
, we arrive at what is called the “restricted warp drive” (
R
-Warp) in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
, since it involves the main restrictions, i.e.,
III.1
, which we concretize in the following: first, we note that the distance
{\rm d}_{\bm{h}}(P_{s},P_{i})
between the center and points in the interior of the warp bubble is translated to
\left|\bm{x}\right|_{\bm{\delta}}\ll R
for two Eulerian observers in the given coordinate system, and similarly, the distance
{\rm d}_{\bm{h}}(P_{s},P_{e})
between the center and points in the exterior is transformed to
\left|\bm{x}\right|_{\bm{\delta}}\gg R
. Second, the Eulerian observer in the interior can “move” with arbitrary speed
\left|\bm{V}_{s}(t)\right|_{\bm{\delta}}
with respect to Eulerian observers in the exterior, hence their speed can be superluminal. Now, we define what we mean by superluminality of
R
-Warp models.

Definition III.8

(Superluminal
R
-Warp)
.

An
R
-Warp is said to be
superluminal
if

\left|\bm{V}_{s}(t)\right|_{\bm{\delta}}>1

(3.27)

for some time interval.

Similar to previous parts, we compute the extrinsic curvature and its principal invariants

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{(i}V_{j)}\,,

(3.28a)

\displaystyle{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{i}V^{i}\,,

(3.28b)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\partial_{i}V^{i}\right)^{2}-\partial^{i}V^{j}\partial_{(i}V_{j)}\right].

(3.28c)

Setting
N=1
in (
3.12
) (or
\psi=0
in (
3.24
)) with (
3.16
) we find

E=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\Big[\partial_{i}\left(V^{i}\partial_{j}V^{j}-V^{j}\partial_{j}V^{i}\right)-2\Omega^{2}\Big].

(3.29)

The energy and momentum constraints (
2.62
) read, respectively,

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=8\pi GE\,,

(3.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\mathchoice{\Omega^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\Omega^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\Omega^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\Omega^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}

\displaystyle=16\pi GJ_{i}\,,

(3.31)

Using
