(3.35)

Therefore, equations (
3.30
), (
3.31
), (
3.33
)–(
3.35
), are the Einstein and the conservation equations for
R
-Warp models.

Remark III.9
.

We see a pattern in (
3.33
) which naturally comes out by a straightforward rewriting: the prefactors of the first terms are equal to the number of the corresponding principal invariant (PI), say
\#
PI, multiplied by
{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]\rm{PI}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]
, and the matter terms are
(E-P)
times
(\rm{P{\rm I}-{\rm I}})[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]
(with
({\rm I}-{\rm I})[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]\equiv 1
), multiplied with factors of
4\pi G
times
(4-\#\rm{PI})
, e.g., if PI=
{\rm II}
, then,
\#\rm{PI}=2
, and the first term is
2{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]\,{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]
and
(\rm{PI-{\rm I}})[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]={\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]
. This structure is easily seen when one considers shear-free
R
-Warp models (see also
Section

III.6.3
and
Theorem

IV.16
).

Remark III.10
.

Equations (7) and (5) in
Lentz 2021a
and
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
, respectively, are the result of
inserting (
3.28c
) into (
3.30
), written in components, where the connection to
{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]
and its role in the formalism is not as clear as in our formulation.

Remark III.11
.

The operator
\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\bm{V}}
is nothing but the
material
(or
convective
, or
Lagrangian
) derivative (usually denoted by
{\mathrm{d}}/{\mathrm{d}}t
), when applied on scalar fields, that is well-known in continuum mechanics (see, e.g.,
Truesdell and Toupin 1960
) and other fields (e.g.,
Buchert et al. 2020
).

Remark III.12
.

In this case, the spatial covariant derivative reduces to the partial derivative. However, one could use a flat metric with its associated covariant derivative
D_{i}
, and get (almost) the same equations since
[D_{i},D_{j}]=0
holds.

Remark III.13
.

Under assumptions of
R1
+
R2
+
R3R3b
, we find
{\bm{u}}=(1,{\bm{V}})
and
{\bm{u}}^{\flat}=(-1,\bm{0})
, hence
{\theta}_{ij}=\Theta_{ij}:=\partial_{(i}V_{j)}=-{\mathcal{K}}_{ij}
, i.e., the principal scalar invariants (as introduced in
Section

I.1
) of the extrinsic curvature are identical (up to sign changes) to the principal scalar invariants of the expansion tensor.

III.6.1
Zero-expansion
R
-Warp models

The Natário zero-expansion warp drive is a subclass of
R
-Warp spacetimes.
13
13

13

To be more precise, one has to assume
R4
and add the condition (
3.37
).

In this case, we have
\mathcal{K}={\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]=0
, hence
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=\bm{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]=-\left|\bm{\mathcal{A}}\right|_{\bm{h}}^{2}/2
, which together with (
3.30
) implies the violation of the WEC (see
Theorem

IV.32
). Moreover, from (
3.33a
) we obtain
E=P
.

III.6.2
Coordinate vorticity-free
R
-Warp models

In this case, since
\bm{\Omega}=\bm{0}
and the spatial hypersurfaces are
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by the Poincaré lemma we have

V_{i}=\partial_{i}\phi\,,

(3.36)

for sufficiently differentiable function
\phi
on
\mathcal{M}
(cf.
32
below). Moreover, from (
3.31
) it follows that
\bm{J}=\bm{0}
, which is observed in
(
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
, Section 3.1)
(cf., also,
17
below). It turns out that the Alcubierre warp drive in this case reduces to Minkowski spacetime (see
Theorem

III.15
).

III.6.3
Shear-free
R
-Warp models

In this case,
\mathcal{A}_{ij}=0
which implies
\mathcal{K}_{ij}=\mathcal{K}\delta_{ij}/3
, and hence
{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]={\rm I}^{2}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]/3
and
{\rm III}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]={\rm I}^{3}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]/27
. This means that all three equations in (
3.33
) are equivalent. Moreover, by (
3.34
) we have
\Pi_{ij}=0
. Finally, from (
3.32
) and the momentum constraint (
2.55
) it follows that
{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]=\mathcal{K}
is a harmonic function (see
Theorem

IV.16
for a consequence).

III.7
Natário metric

As mentioned in
Section

III.6
, the Natário metric is a subclass of
R
-Warp models with a further condition.
In fact,
Natário 2002
slightly generalized the Alcubierre metric (see
Section

III.8
below).
The shift vector
\bm{N}
is assumed to have the following property: components of
\bm{N}
should be bounded smooth functions, i.e.,

N^{i}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathcal{M})\,.

(3.37)

Hence, the only differences between the Alcubierre and the Natário proposals are that
\bm{N}
has three non-vanishing components in general, and Natário does not prescribe a specific form of the shift vector (e.g., a form function
f
which plays the role of a window function, cf.
Section

III.8
below). The condition (
3.37
) assures that the spacetime is globally hyperbolic (see
Section

IV.1
, in particular
Theorem

IV.3
) which forbids the existence of closed timelike curves (CTCs) whose existence and role we do not analyze in this paper, and refer the reader to, e.g.,
Shoshany and Snodgrass 2024
;
Shoshany 2019
;
Everett 1996
.

III.7.1
Natário with 1D shift vector

For this case, we have

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,,

(3.38)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})=-V(t,\bm{x})\delta^{i}_{1}\,,

under which (
3.28
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{(i}V\delta^{1}_{j)}\,,

(3.39a)

\displaystyle{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{1}V\,,

(3.39b)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\left[\left(\partial_{2}V\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{3}V\right)^{2}\right]\,.

(3.39c)

The only nonvanishing components of the extrinsic curvature are

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{11}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{1}V={\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]=\mathcal{K}\,,

(3.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}V\,;\quad i=2,3\,.

The coordinate vorticity becomes
\Omega_{ij}=\delta^{1}_{[i}\partial_{j]}V
with the only nonvanishing components being

\Omega_{1i}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}V=-\mathcal{K}_{1i}\,;\quad i=2,3\,,

(3.41)

which results in

\Omega^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left[\left(\partial_{2}V\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{3}V\right)^{2}\right]=-{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]\,,

(3.42)

since the first term in bracket in (
3.29
) vanishes.
This has the immediate implication coming from the energy constraint equation (
2.62
):

E=-\frac{1}{32\pi G}\left[\left(\partial_{2}V\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{3}V\right)^{2}\right]=-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{8\pi G}\leq 0\,.

(3.43)

This shows that the energy density is non-positive, hence the WEC is violated (cf.
Section

IV.8.1
).

Remark III.14
.

The relation (
3.43
) shows that the coordinate vorticity is the central kinematic quantity for the Natário warp drive with
1
-dimensional shift vector, including the Alcubierre metric. Nonetheless, it does not mean that if
\bm{\Omega}=\bm{0}
, then the spacetime is trivial, although this is certainly the case for the specific form function in Alcubierre warp drive (see
Theorem

III.15
). The correlation between the coordinate vorticity and the Eulerian energy density, to our knowledge, has been first observed in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
(cf.
III.7
).

III.8
Alcubierre metric

Assuming all restrictions, i.e.,
R1
+
R2
+
R3R3b
+
R4
+
R5
,
we retrieve the Alcubierre metric, proposed first in
Alcubierre 1994
, by taking the same metric as in
Section

III.7.1
with a special form for the coordinate velocity, i.e.,

\displaystyle h_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,,

(3.44)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle N^{i}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-V^{i}(t,\bm{x})=-V_{s}(t)[f(r_{s})-1]\delta^{i}_{1}\,,

with
r_{s}:=(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})^{1/2}
.

The reader might wonder why the metric given by (
3.44
) is not the same as the original metric given by
Alcubierre 1994
. The different representation of the coordinate velocity in (
3.44
) is due to the fact that we defined the metric of the
R
-Warp models in (
3.26
) in terms of the comoving coordinate with
x\equiv x_{{\rm Alc.}}-x_{s}(t)
, where
x_{s}(t)
is the trajectory of the spaceship and

V_{s}(t)=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x_{s}(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\,,

is the velocity of the spaceship, located at the center of the warp bubble. The reason for this representation is to cope with
III.1
motivated by
Natário 2002
.

The warp bubble in this special case is described by a function
f:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]
, which is a window function (called “form function” in
Alcubierre 1994
), assumed to be spherically symmetric and has the following form

f(r_{s})=\frac{\tanh(\sigma(r_{s}+R))-\tanh(\sigma(r_{s}-R))}{2\tanh(\sigma R)}\,,

(3.45)

where
R>0
and
\sigma>0
are two arbitrary parameters interpreted as the radius of the warp bubble and inversely proportional to the bubble wall thickness, respectively. The function
f(r_{s})
has the general property that it, approximately, is equal to one in a neighborhood of the origin of the warp bubble and it vanishes outside it
Alcubierre 1994
, i.e.,

\lim_{\sigma\rightarrow\infty}f(r_{s})=\begin{cases}1\,;&-R\leq r_{s}\leq R\,,\\
0\,,&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(3.46)

The form function
f(r_{s})
guarantees that the shift vector satisfies (
3.37
), and the spacetime is asymptotically flat (see
IV.15
).
Therefore, both the exterior and the interior of the warp bubble are flat.

Then, (
3.28
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-V_{s}\partial_{(i}f\delta^{1}_{j)}=-V_{s}f^{\prime}\delta^{1}_{(i}\partial_{j)}r_{s}\,,

(3.47a)

\displaystyle{\rm I}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{r_{s}}V_{s}f^{\prime}\,,

(3.47b)

\displaystyle{\rm II}[\bm{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{y^{2}+z^{2}}{4r_{s}^{2}}V_{s}^{2}\left(f^{\prime}\right)^{2}=-\Omega^{2}\,,

(3.47c)

where
f^{\prime}\equiv{\mathrm{d}}f/{\mathrm{d}}r_{s}
.
Hence,

E=-\frac{1}{32\pi G}\frac{y^{2}+z^{2}}{r_{s}^{2}}V_{s}^{2}\left(f^{\prime}\right)^{2}\leq 0\,,

(3.48)

which indicates the violation of the WEC, as it is mentioned already by
Alcubierre 1994
.

We finish this section with the following theorem which was announced in
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
and
Section

III.6.2
.

Theorem III.15
.

Coordinate vorticity-free Alcubierre warp drive is Minkowski.

Proof.

If
\bm{\Omega}=\bm{0}
, then from (
3.47c
) and hence (
3.48
), it follows that either
V_{s}=0
or
f^{\prime}=0
. In either case, we obtain
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=\bm{0}
(or even stronger) which finishes the proof.
∎

IV
Demystification of warp-drive spacetimes

This section examines some profound misconceptions, misunderstandings, and mistakes together with their consequences in the warp-drive literature. To enhance readability and facilitate access, this section makes frequent use of Remarks and Errors.

IV.1
Global hyperbolicity of warp-drive spacetimes

In this section we examine the claim that the Alcubierre warp-drive spacetime is
globally hyperbolic
. While
Natário 2002
defines a warp-drive spacetime to be globally hyperbolic (although, as we shall argue below, it puts a constraint on the coordinate velocity), this property is justified wrongly by
Alcubierre 1994
, and it is often repeated in the existing literature without critical examination. In fact, one needs extra assumptions to assure the global hyperbolicity of the Alcubierre warp drive. Global hyperbolicity has, in any case, a severe consequence, as we shall see below.

We start with the Error that appeared in the original work by
Alcubierre 1994
.

Error 1
.

Alcubierre 1994
claimed that every spacetime whose metric is locally described by its ADM form of the metric is globally hyperbolic as long as the spatial metric,
h_{ij}
in our notation, is positive definite for all
t\in I\subset\mathbb{R}
. This is not true in general as one can construct counterexamples to this statement, e.g., a Schwarzschild spacetime with negative (ADM) mass (cf., e.g.,
(
Wald 1984
, Section 11.2)
) or the anti-de Sitter spacetime (cf., e.g.,
(
Choquet-Bruhat 2008
, Section V.4.6)
) fail to be globally hyperbolic.
Theorem

IV.3
below also shows that the positive-definiteness of
h_{ij}
alone does not guarantee that the spacetime is globally hyperbolic. This is often repeated or assumed in almost all subsequent works so that we do not list them here.
14
14

14

For example,
Santiago et al. 2022
state: “The warp-drive spacetime is by construction globally hyperbolic.”

Determining the global hyperbolicity of a given spacetime is, in general, a difficult task. Nevertheless, there are some conditions under which one can prove it. For this purpose, we suggest using a theorem by
Choquet-Bruhat and Cotsakis 2002
(see also
(
Choquet-Bruhat 2008
, Section 11.4)
). But, before proceeding, it is helpful to repeat the (informal) definition of the global hyperbolicity.

Definition IV.1

(Global hyperbolicity)
.

A spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\bm{g})
is globally hyperbolic if it admits a Cauchy surface
\Sigma
, i.e., a spacelike surface in
\mathcal{M}
such that each causal curve without end points intersects it once and only once.

Remark IV.2
.

The more technical definitions of the global hyperbolicity require some notions from the causality theory on Lorentzian manifolds. We refer the reader to
(
Minguzzi 2019
, Section 4.5.4)
and
(
Chruściel 2020
, Section 2.8)
for further details and recent developments on this definition.

Theorem IV.3

(Choquet-Bruhat–Cotsakis, 2002)
.

Any Lorentzian spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\bm{g})
with
\mathcal{M}\cong\Sigma_{t}\times I
and
I=(t_{0},\infty)
, where
\Sigma_{t}
is an
3
-dimensional Riemannian manifold for each
t\in I
, and
\bm{g}
is a Lorentzian metric which has the ADM form as in (
2.6
), is globally hyperbolic if

(i)

the spacetime is time-oriented by increasing
t
,

(ii)

the lapse function
N
is bounded below and above by positive numbers
N_{m}
and
N_{M}
, i.e.,
0<N_{m}\leq N\leq N_{M}
,

(iii)

the metric
h_{ij}
is a complete Riemannian metric on
\Sigma_{t}
uniformly bounded below for all
t\in I
by a metric
\mathfrak{g}_{ij}=h_{ij}(t_{0})
, i.e., there exists a positive constant
A>0
such that for vectors
\bm{w}\in\mathfrak{X}\left(\Sigma_{t}\right)
we have
A\mathfrak{g}_{ij}w^{i}w^{j}\leq h_{ij}w^{i}w^{j}
,

(iv)

the
\bm{h}
-norm of the shift vector is uniformly bounded by some positive number, i.e.,
\left|\bm{N}\right|_{\bm{h}}=\left(h_{ij}N^{i}N^{j}\right)^{1/2}\leq\bar{N}
for some
\bar{N}>0
.

Remark IV.4
.

Condition
(i)
either prevents possible emergence of horizons or pathologies in the given foliation, and hence puts a bound on the lapse function and the shift vector through
\left|\bm{N}\right|_{\bm{h}}<N
(which,
per se
, does not prohibit superluminality in the context of warp-drive spacetimes, see also
(
Gourgoulhon 2012
, Section 5.2.2)
) that in combination with condition
(ii)
results in
\left|\bm{N}\right|_{\bm{h}}<N_{M}
, thereby implying condition
(iv)
, or it ensures that, in the presence of horizons, we restrict ourselves to the exterior region, or more precisely, the
domain of outer communication
(DOC):
I^{+}(\mathscr{I}^{-})\cap I^{-}(\mathscr{I}^{+})
(see, e.g.,
(
Chruściel 2020
, Section 3.1)
for a precise definition).

Remark IV.5
.

Conditions
(ii)
and
(iii)
are evidently satisfied for the
R
-Warp models.
One needs to check conditions
(i)
and
(iv)
. Condition
(i)
is satisfied globally only for the subluminal case where no horizon emerges (cf., e.g.,
Natário 2002
;
Hiscock 1997
;
Finazzi et al. 2009
), or in the DOC for the superluminal case where two horizons exist or form (cf. the Penrose diagrams in
(
Finazzi et al. 2009
, Figures 2 and 3)
). Conversely, if condition
(i)
holds globally, then from
IV.4
it follows that
\left|\bm{V}\right|_{\bm{\delta}}<1
, i.e., the
R
-Warp model is subluminal.
Condition
(iv)
, however, is not mentioned by
Alcubierre 1994
, but
Natário 2002
added this condition later explicitly, i.e., condition (
3.37
).

As it is somewhat clear from the above, one can show that it is impossible to “construct” a globally hyperbolic superluminal
R
-Warp model.
We adopt the definitions of an
eternal
and a
dynamic
warp drive introduced by
Finazzi et al. 2009
to make precise what we mean by constructing a warp drive.

Definition IV.6

(Eternal and dynamic warp-drive spacetimes)
.

An
eternal
warp drive is the one with a time-independent
\bm{V}_{s}
(hence
\bm{V}
), whereas a
dynamic
warp drive has a time-dependent
\bm{V}_{s}(t)
in such a way that it starts from zero and reaches some final value by introducing a “switching factor,” say,
S_{w}(t)
.
The latter is equivalent to saying that one constructs a warp drive.

Theorem IV.7

(Global hyperbolicity of
R
-Warp models)
.

It is impossible to construct a superluminal
R
-Warp model that is globally hyperbolic.

Remark IV.8
.

Although
Finazzi et al. 2009
defined
S_{w}(t)
to be
C^{0}
, another (perhaps more desired) possibility is to opt for a step function as a switching factor that obviously provides more problems. However, as we shall show in the proof below, even for a
C^{0}
switching factor, superluminal
R
-Warp models cannot be constructed if one requires them to be globally hyperbolic at the same time.

Proof of
Theorem

IV.7
.

If the given
R
-Warp model is subluminal, then, by
IV.5
, it satisfies all conditions of
Theorem

IV.3
, hence it is globally hyperbolic. Conversely, if it is globally hyperbolic in the given foliation, then it cannot contain horizons which is only the case if it is subluminal.
If it is superluminal, as mentioned in
IV.5
, it allows for two horizons. Now, if it is an eternal
R
-Warp model, then no Cauchy horizon arises (cf.
Finazzi et al. 2009
), but only the exterior region is globally hyperbolic. In any case, an eternal
R
-Warp model, as the name suggests, are not constructed, hence we are left with the possibility of construction of a superluminal dynamic
R
-Warp model. In this case, Cauchy horizons appear (cf.
Finazzi et al. 2009
; see also
Low 1999
), so that not even the exterior (the DOC) is globally hyperbolic. This is independent of the regularity of the switching factor
S_{w}(t)
. We hence conclude that a globally hyperbolic
R
-Warp model is necessarily subluminal.
∎

Remark IV.9
.

An implication of
Theorem

IV.7
is that one may omit the global hyperbolicity when assuming a reasonable hypothesis for the construction of warp drive as intended.
This, from a philosophical point of view, makes sense; indeed, if one could manipulate the spacetime however one wants, then the theory could not be fully deterministic (see, e.g.,
Smeenk and Wüthrich 2021
for a discussion on determinism in GR).

Remark IV.10
.

Our result is, in a sense, complementary to, but distinct from that by
Low 1999
, who in an interesting work utilizes the initial-value problem to address the challenge of modeling
agency
within a deterministic framework by defining a “decision” as a localized modification of initial data on a subset of a future Cauchy surface.
He then proves that the spacetime region outside the
domain of dependence
of this decision remains isometric to the original, i.e., even if an agent has the “freedom” to choose (or change) the initial data at a point
p\in\mathcal{M}
, that choice (or change) is strictly bounded by the domain of dependence of that slice, hence, the “decision” cannot influence any part of the Universe faster than the speed of light. Consequently, Low concludes in
(
Low 1999
, Theorem 4.2)
that it is impossible to reach a destination any sooner than light would allow if the matter involved is “physically reasonable”—a matter field defined as satisfying a
symmetric hyperbolic system
and the dominant energy condition (DEC). Therefore, even if an observer can “choose” initial conditions, the deterministic evolution of nonexotic matter ensures that a warp drive cannot bypass the universal speed limit (associated to the ray cone of the symmetric hyperbolic system, or the light cone; see
IV.38
for further discussion). Although our result for
R
-Warp may seem to be more general, since we did not assume any energy conditions,
Low 1999
proved his result for any notion of warp drive which does not have any restriction except from energy conditions.

Remark IV.11
.

Theorem

IV.7
implies that either
\bm{V}
(hence
\bm{V}_{s}(t)
) should be already (i.e., eternally) superluminal (tachyonic) before “turning on” the warp drive, or it can never be superluminal if one assumes the global hyperbolicity. This was first pointed out by
Krasnikov 1998
using causality theory, and also somehow highlighted, but only from a qualitative perspective, by
Coule 1998
. In addition, following the work by
Krasnikov 1998
,
Everett and Roman 1997
show that a spaceship crew cannot create or control a warp bubble following a “simple argument”: since the bubble’s front edge is spacelike separated from the ship’s center, hence any signal or action taken by the captain is restricted to their future light cone, because a forward-emitted photon eventually reaches a point where its speed equals the bubble’s velocity, meaning it remains at rest relative to the bubble and never reaches the front edge. Consequently, the crew has no way to create or control it, meaning the bubble would have to be constructed in advance by an external observer whose own light cone already covers the entire intended path. See the following Error.

Error 2
.

The facts mentioned in the previous Remark contradict the assumption of possibility of “building” a warp drive starting from an “approximately Minkowski spacetime” reflected, for example, in the works by
Lentz 2021b
;
Lentz 2021a
, and
Helmerich et al. 2024
, if one insists on the global hyperbolicity of the given spacetime.

Remark IV.12
.

2
is an example of violation of
I.1
; a concept is often used by supporters of physical warp drives without exploring why, and what exactly the consequences are.

IV.2
Asymptotic flatness

In the literature, warp-drive spacetimes are assumed to fulfill
R4
, i.e., to be asymptotically flat. However, this is occasionally assumed without precise investigation which led to some misunderstandings and wrong results.
Thus, we provide a simplified and specific definition of asymptotically flat spacetimes, sufficient for our purposes, and refer the reader to, e.g.,
York 1978
;
Straumann 2013
;
Gourgoulhon 2012
;
Barzegar et al. 2017
;
Chruściel 2013
for further details and subtleties.

Definition IV.13

(Asymptotically flat slices)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\bm{g})
be a Lorentzian manifold and
\Sigma
a complete oriented 3-dimensional hypersurface. Then,
\Sigma
is said to be asymptotically flat iff the following holds

(
i
)

there is a compact set
\mathcal{C}\subset\Sigma
such that
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{C}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B(\mathscr{R})
where
B(\mathscr{R})
is a coordinate ball of radius
\mathscr{R}>0
,

(
ii
)

in local standard coordinates
(x^{i})
on
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{C}
obtained from
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B(\mathscr{R})
, the components of the metric on
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{C}
satisfy the following falloff conditions

h_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-1})\,,\quad\partial_{k}h_{ij}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-2})\,,

(
iii
)

in the same coordinates, the components of the second fundamental form (extrinsic curvature) of
\Sigma
on
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{C}
satisfy the following falloff conditions

\mathcal{K}_{ij}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-2})\,,\quad\partial_{k}\mathcal{K}_{ij}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-3})\,,

when
r\rightarrow+\infty
, where
r:=\left|\bm{x}\right|_{\bm{\delta}}
.

Remark IV.14
.

Since the asymptotic flatness for
R
-Warp models are always discussed with regard to the
\{t=\mbox{\rm const}.\}
-foliations in (
3.26
) which are also taken to be Cauchy surfaces, we can reformulate
IV.13
by considering asymptotically flat
spacetimes
, i.e., spacetimes with
\mathcal{M}=\Sigma_{t}\times I
and
I=(t_{0},\infty)
where
(\Sigma_{t},\bm{h})
are complete 3-dimensional Riemannian manifolds for all
t\in I
, and replace
\Sigma
by
\Sigma_{t}
. This definition, however, contains already global hyperbolicity, whereas
IV.13
does not presume it. Note that, in a spacetime sense, we further have
\mathcal{L}_{n}K_{ij}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-3})
in the same coordinate system.

Based on
IV.14
, hereafter whenever we refer to an
R
-Warp model, we mean that the
\{t=\mbox{\rm const}.\}
-slices are asymptotically flat in the sense prescribed in
IV.13
.

Remark IV.15
.

The Alcubierre spacetime (and any other warp-drive spacetime with (
3.45
)) is asymptotically flat; indeed, for the Alcubierre shape function (
3.45
) and its derivative we have
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(e^{-2r})
, which in turn implies
\mathcal{K}_{ij},\partial_{k}\mathcal{K}_{ij}\in\mathcal{O}(e^{-2r})
which certainly decay faster than required.

The ignorance of the precise definition of asymptotic flatness
IV.13
has some serious consequences which we will mention in conjunction with the errors regarding the notion of the ADM energy (see Errors
6
and
29
).
Asymptotic flatness puts some strong restrictions on a given
R
-Warp model. For example, as we will show in
Theorem

IV.19
, it dictates
R
-Warp models to have vanishing ADM energy. Furthermore, we prove in the following that for the coordinate vorticity-free
R
-Warp models and the shear-free
R
-Warp models, introduced in
Section

III.6.2
and
Section

III.6.3
, respectively, the asymptotic flatness forces them to be Minkowski.

Theorem IV.16
.

Shear-free
R
-Warp models are Minkowski.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{A}_{ij}=0
, we have
\mathcal{K}_{ij}=\mathcal{K}\delta_{ij}/3
, and from the momentum constraint equation (
2.55
) we find
J_{i}=-12\pi G\partial_{i}\mathcal{K}
, which in turn in combination with (
3.32
) yields
\Delta_{\bm{\delta}}\mathcal{K}=0
. Now, since the
R
-Warp models are asymptotically flat by definition, then
\mathcal{K}
decays as
\mathcal{O}(r^{-2})
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see
IV.13
below), which means that it is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as
\mathcal{K}
is continuous. Now, by Liouville’s Theorem (cf., e.g.,
(
Evans 2010
, Section 2.2.3)
) we conclude that
\mathcal{K}
is (spatially) constant (hence identically zero because it decays to zero, cf. also
Theorem

IV.17
below). Therefore,
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=\bm{0}
, and all its principal invariants vanish, resulting in
E=0
, and by (
3.33
) in
P=0
. Finally, from (
3.34
) it follows
\Pi_{ij}=0
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem IV.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\bm{g})
be an
R
-Warp model. If the shift vector is the gradient of a harmonic function on the spatial slices, then
(\mathcal{M},\bm{g})
is Minkowski.

Proof.

We start with (
3.36
), i.e.,
V_{i}=\partial_{i}\phi
, hence
\mathcal{K}_{ij}=-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi
. Since
\phi
is harmonic on the spatial slices, the mean curvature vanishes, i.e.,
\mathcal{K}=\Delta_{\bm{\delta}}\phi=0
. Since the slices are asymptotically flat
\phi
decays fast enough, hence it is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. By Liouville’s Theorem we conclude that
\phi
is zero (cf.
Theorem

IV.16
), resulting in
V_{i}=0
, hence finishing the proof.
∎

Error 3
.

Lentz 2021a
argues that an
R
-Warp model with an “elliptic” shift vector is considered in the literature, i.e., a warp-drive model with a shift vector of the form (
3.36
) that satisfies
\Delta_{\bm{\delta}}\phi=0
. But,
Theorem

IV.17
shows that such a spacetime is identically Minkowski, and not a zero-expansion Natário warp drive.

We finish this section by demonstrating a misunderstanding which appeared several times in the warp-drive literature.

Error 4
.

Bobrick and Martire 2021
talk about the “truncation” of the gravitational field in the Alcubierre or Natário spacetimes, which, as criticized correctly by
Santiago et al. 2022
, is wrong since the shape function (
3.45
) does not have a compact support, but it falls off very fast (cf.
IV.15
).
Helmerich et al. 2024
point out this mistake, without acknowledging the critique by
Santiago et al. 2022
, or referring to the mistake done by
Bobrick and Martire 2021
. The same misunderstanding entered the work by
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
. See also
27
for a related mistake.

IV.3
Energy and mass in
R
-Warp spacetimes

In this section, we shall clarify the misconception around the notion of (total) energy and ADM energy, and their role in
R
-Warp models.

In most of the works on warp-drive spacetimes which are
R
-Warp models, one attributes a total energy (or mass) to these spacetimes, using a naive, Newtonian-like definition for the energy (in the given foliation)

E_{\text{tot}}:=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}T_{{\mu\nu}}n^{\mu}n^{\nu}{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}E{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x\,,

(4.1)

with
E
being the Eulerian energy density defined in (
2.27a
).
Here, we argue that this notion of energy (mass) is ambiguous, or at best is nonunique, for
R
-Warp models and therefore
cannot
fulfill the role it is meant to play in the literature, i.e., it does
not
necessarily represent the “total mass” of the warp bubble. One of these ambiguities is related to the misunderstanding about the ADM energy. In the warp-drive literature there are works which speculate about the ADM energy for
R
-Warp models, but, as it turns out easily by just applying the definitions properly, it is zero for such spacetimes, whereas
E_{\text{tot}}
is not, in general. However,
E_{\text{tot}}
in (
4.1
) is occasionally taken to be the same as the ADM energy (cf.
IV.24
). Therefore, to present the results appropriately, we will start with the notion of the ADM quantities (hence the ADM energy).

We shall begin with a basic but important fact: the ADM energy is defined only for asymptotically flat spacetimes. Unfortunately, this seemingly trivial and basic fact has often been misunderstood in the literature on warp-drive spacetimes.

For this purpose, we define the ADM energy, or more generally, the ADM
4
-momentum, thus, in particular, distinguishing between the ADM mass and the ADM energy (cf.
Arnowitt et al. 2008b
).
15
15

15

Sometimes in the literature the ADM energy and ADM mass are used interchangeably. We will stick to the precise definition repeated here.

Definition IV.18
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\bm{g})
be asymptotically flat in the sense it is defined in
IV.13
. Then, the total energy
E_{\mathrm{ADM}}
and momentum
\bm{P}_{\mathrm{ADM}}
contained in
\Sigma_{t}
is encoded in the ADM
4
-momentum

\bm{p}_{\mathrm{ADM}}=\left(E_{\mathrm{ADM}},\bm{P}_{\mathrm{ADM}}\right)\,,

(4.2)

with

E_{\mathrm{ADM}}:=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\rm S}_{r}}\left(\partial^{i}h_{ij}-\partial_{j}h\right)\frac{x^{j}}{r}{\mathrm{d}}{\rm S}\,,

(4.3)

and

\left(\bm{P}_{\mathrm{ADM}}\right)^{i}:=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\rm S}_{r}}\left(\mathchoice{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathcal{K}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}-\mathcal{K}\mathchoice{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\delta^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}\right)\frac{x^{j}}{r}{\mathrm{d}}{\rm S}\,,

(4.4)

where
\partial^{i}
is defined as in (
3.10
),
h:=\delta^{ij}h_{ij}
, and
{\rm S}_{r}
denotes a sphere of radius
r
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B(\mathscr{R})
and
{\mathrm{d}}{\rm S}
is the volume element on
{\rm S}_{r}
induced by the flat metric, all for the coordinates
(x^{i})
defined in
IV.13
. Moreover, the ADM mass is

m_{\text{ADM}}^{2}:=E_{\text{ADM}}^{2}-|\bm{P_{\text{ADM}}}|^{2}_{\bm{\delta}}\,,

(4.5)

We continue with a trivial but often overlooked result.

Theorem IV.19
.

Consider an
R
-Warp model spacetime. Then,

(
i
)

the ADM energy vanishes, i.e.,
E_{\text{ADM}}=0
,

(
ii
)

the ADM momentum does not vanish in general, i.e.,
\bm{P_{\text{ADM}}}\neq\bm{0}
, hence in combination of the previous fact we have
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{ADM}}=\left(0,\bm{P_{\text{ADM}}}\right)
,

(
iii
)

if the
R
-Warp model spacetime reduces to the Alcubierre one, then both the ADM energy and ADM momentum vanish, i.e.,
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{ADM}}^{\mathrm{Alc.}}=\bm{0}
.

Proof.

The result for the ADM energy follows immediately by setting
h_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
in (
4.3
). For the ADM momentum the result depends on the falloff conditions of the second fundamental form. It is, however, zero for the special case of the Alcubierre metric due to the rapid decay of the second fundamental form (see
IV.15
).
∎

Theorem

IV.19
has an important consequence which is related to the famous positive energy (mass) theorem
Witten 1981
;
Schoen and Yau 1981
, and also a consequence of asymptotic flatness (
R4
).

Theorem IV.20

(Positive energy theorem)
.

Consider an
R
-Warp model spacetime. Then, the positive energy theorem, which relies on the DEC being satisfied, dictates that the spacetime must be Minkowski.

Remark IV.21
.

If one drops the DEC assumption, some serious physical ambiguities arise, as shown in
IV.22
(see also
IV.37
).

We do not wish to list all misuses of the notion of the ADM mass in the literature. In the following we list some of these occurrences in terms of Errors.

Error 5
.

The left-hand side of Equation (11.26) in a work by
Alcubierre and Lobo 2017
is clearly misleading, if not wrong. Again, the authors did not pay attention to the criteria in
IV.13
(cf.
Theorem

IV.19
). This appears to originate from the work of
Lobo and Visser 2004a
(see also
Lobo and Visser 2004b
), where the authors introduce the concept of “ADM mass due to stress-energy” (see
(
Lobo and Visser 2004a
, Equation (67))
). This notion is not well-defined, and serves as the basis for their speculation about the “compensation of the ADM mass of the spaceship by the ADM mass due to the stress-energy” and the violation of the energy conditions (
(
Lobo and Visser 2004a
, Section 2.1)
). Moreover,
Lobo and Visser 2004a
take the ADM energy to be equal to
E_{\text{tot}}
given in (
4.1
) (see
9
). See also
29
for a related mistake.

Error 6
.

The work by
Schuster et al. 2023
reflects several misconceptions regarding the notion of the mass and energy in GR, especially the ADM energy;
16
16

16

Although the subtleties of this issue were expounded in
(
Schuster et al. 2023
, Section (2.3))
.
indeed,
Schuster et al. 2023
mistakenly attribute a nonvanishing ADM energy (referred to as the ADM mass by the authors) to the Natário warp drive by considering the “zero-vorticity warp bubble,” since “this is the form most appropriate to make the appearance of notions of mass in a warp drive explicit, as it can be easily compared to Schwarzschild in Painlevé–Gullstrand form,” and so they construct what they call “Schwarzschild-based warp drive.” However, this strategy relies on several misunderstandings that lead to wrong consequences which we list below:

(
i
)

By
Theorem

IV.19
, the ADM energy of generic asymptotically flat Natário warp drives (i.e.,
R
-Warp models) vanishes, and this
cannot
be avoided, and it has nothing to do with the vorticity as the authors claim. This misunderstanding occurred already by the same authors in
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Section VII.C)
, where they speculated that only Alcubierre and zero-expansion Natário warp drives have vanishing ADM energy. Then, the parameter
M
in
(
Schuster et al. 2023
, Equation (2.11))
is at best ambiguous. Unless, the authors mean that the
\{t=\mbox{\rm const}.\}
-slices there are not asymptotically flat, and only after a transformation to an asymptotically flat foliation the ADM mass of the spacetime can be computed. In any case, this shows a lack of precision (see
(iv)
below).

(
ii
)

The foliation associated to the Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates is not asymptotically flat (since the third condition in
IV.13
is not satisfied),
making the concept of ADM energy irrelevant in this context. This is a classic pitfall against which one is warned (cf., e.g.,
(
Gourgoulhon 2012
, Example 8.2)
and
(
Baumgarte and Shapiro 2010
, Section 3.5)
), and it is reflected
in the part where authors write “[…] finiteness of the ADM mass places mild constraints on the fall-off of the metric components.”

(
iii
)

Comparing the Schwarzschild metric in the Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates with that of the Natário warp drive is wrong if one assumes that the Natário warp drive is asymptotically flat.

(
iv
)

Although the ADM mass of the Schwarzschild metric in the foliation associated to the Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates has no meaning, other notions of mass or energy (such as the Komar or the Misner–Sharp mass, see, e.g.,
Barzegar et al. 2017
;
Beig 1978
;
Misner and Sharp 1964
) can be used to infer the ADM energy of the Schwarzschild spacetime in this foliation. However, in general, this
cannot
be done for an asymptotically flat Natário warp drive with respect to foliations that are not asymptotically flat, because the metric is, in particular, not stationary nor spherically symmetric.

(
v
)

Schuster et al. 2023
consider the Misner–Sharp mass for the Schwarzschild-based warp drive and conclude
m(r)=m[1+\mathcal{O}(r^{-n})]
for some
n>0
, which is again wrong, because, firstly, the Misner–Sharp mass is defined only for the spherically symmetric spacetimes (cf., e.g.,
Hayward 1996
, see also
Abreu and Visser 2010
by one of the authors), whereas the Schwarzschild-based warp drive is not spherically symmetric,
17
17

17

Although the same authors point out a similar misunderstanding by others in
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Appendix A)
, they make the same mistake here.
secondly, even if one naively computes it, it would not be of the form claimed by the authors (it also would not be invariant as it depends on coordinates), and
m
is not “simply” the ADM mass.

(
vi
)

Finally, the use of what is supposed to be the ADM energy to show the violation of the NEC is not valid, as it is not defined here (cf.
IV.36
).

Consequently, most of the speculations involving the ADM energy in the context of
R
-Warp models should be re-evaluated. This includes not only the work by
Schuster et al. 2023
, but also the ones by
Clough et al. 2024
, where the authors related the “true ADM mass” to “causal disconnectedness” of the Alcubierre warp drive, and
Fuchs et al. 2024
which suffers further from a vague formulation so that one cannot decide if the speculated metric is asymptotically flat or not (see also
9
).

Error 7
.

Santiago et al. 2022
give an inadequate falloff for “zero-vorticity warp drives” (cf.
Section

III.6.2
) while assuming that the given spatial slice is asymptotically flat, hence the ADM energy would be zero. Indeed, the falloff rate of the second fundamental form is
\mathcal{O}(r^{-3/2})
which is not enough and does not satisfy
IV.13
(the same mistake is done by
Shoshany and Snodgrass 2024
). Therefore, the ADM energy in this case in not defined at all, unless they mean the ADM mass that is well-defined in another foliation. Even in that case the given falloff rate is insufficient.

Remark IV.22
.

Although, by
Theorem

IV.19
, the ADM energy vanishes for
R
-Warp models, the ADM momentum can, in general, be nonzero which leads to
m_{\text{ADM}}^{2}=-|\bm{P_{\text{ADM}}}|^{2}_{\bm{\delta}}
, resulting in an imaginary ADM mass. This, in the quantum field theory, corresponds to off-shell particles (virtual particles), in particular the tachyonic ones (cf., e.g.,
Feinberg 1967
,
(
Fayngold 2002
, Section 8)
,
(
Gourgoulhon 2013
, Remarks 2.3 and 4.5)
, and references therein). However, instead of particles, in this case we are dealing with an isolated system, whose interpretation is not clear to us. It is, however, to some extent reminiscent of the work by
Coule 1998
.
It is even worse if we consider the Alcubierre warp drive, for which we have
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{ADM}}^{\mathrm{Alc.}}=\bm{0}
, i.e., a spacetime which has no nontrivial global quantities at infinity, still carrying some content.
Therefore, it is not clear at all what the meaning of this configuration is, even if we allow for violation of some energy conditions.

Remark IV.23
.

In a sense,
R
-Warp spacetimes are dual to the Schwarzschild spacetime; indeed, for the Schwarzschild spacetime we have
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{ADM}}=\left(m,\bm{0}\right)
where
m
is the mass parameter, whereas for the
R
-Warp spacetime we have
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{ADM}}=\left(0,\bm{P_{\text{ADM}}}\right)
when
\bm{P_{\text{ADM}}}\neq\bm{0}
.

We note that there is no direct relation between the violation of the WEC and the vanishing of the ADM mass, in general.

Error 8
.

Fuchs et al. 2024
speculate that the violation of the energy conditions for the Alcubierre metric (or
R
-Warp models in general) is due to the vanishing of the ADM energy. However, this is not a consequence as there are direct counter examples (see, e.g.,
Frauendiener et al. 1990
;
Saito and Yoshida 2025
).

In the following important Remark, we elaborate on the relation between the ADM energy, the Eulerian energy density, and quantities in the Newtonian limit.

Remark IV.24
.

The classical Newtonian limit can be derived from a weak-field metric in the Newtonian gauge, i.e.,
\bm{g}=-(1+2\Phi){\mathbf{d}}t^{2}+(1-2\Psi)\bm{\gamma}
(assuming no vector and tensor modes). If one assumes that the pressure and the anisotropic stress from the matter content do not source the gravitational field at the Newtonian level, then the two scalar fields
\Psi
and
\Phi
coincide and correspond to the Newtonian gravitational potential which is the solution to the Poisson equation
\Delta_{\bm{\delta}}\Psi=4\pi G\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textbf{N}}}
, where
\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textbf{N}}}=\lim_{c\rightarrow\infty}E
is the Newtonian limit of the Eulerian energy density. A direct consequence is that the ADM energy is related to the Newtonian potential as follows (cf., e.g.,
(
Wald 1984
, Section 11.2)
,
(
Gourgoulhon 2012
, Section 8.3.3)
, and
(
Chruściel 2013
, Section 1.1)
):

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{ADM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi G}\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\rm S}_{r}}\partial_{i}\Psi\,\frac{x^{i}}{r}{\mathrm{d}}{\rm S}

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi G}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Delta_{\bm{\delta}}\Psi\,{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textbf{N}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x\,.

This relation between the ADM energy, the Newtonian potential, and the Newtonian limit of the Eulerian density is however much more subtle for warp-drive spacetimes for several reasons. First, because the
R
-Warp metric (
3.26
) is not written in the Newtonian gauge, one cannot simply read off
\Phi
and
\Psi
from that metric. Second, the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}E{\rm d}^{3}x\not=0
but
E_{\mathrm{ADM}}=0
in general for
R
-Warp shows that the Newtonian limit of the former is not equal to the latter. This highly suggests that, either “
\Phi=\Psi
’’ does not hold anymore in the limit,
18
18

18

Having
\Phi\neq\Psi
in the Newtonian limit would imply that the pressure and the anisotropic stress of the matter content have a nonzero contribution to the Newtonian gravitational potential. For instance, this can be the case for radiative fluids.
or that the Newtonian potential is not sourced by the Newtonian limit of
E
. These two points make the identification and interpretation of the Newtonian gravitational potential, and more generally the Newtonian limit, for warp drives difficult, and a proper investigation of the Newtonian limit of such spacetimes is therefore required. To this end, the use of the covariant “geometric” limit involving Galilean structures seems the most appropriate formalism (see, e.g.,
Künzle 1976
;
Hansen et al. 2020
). In an interesting work, using a similar formalism known as the
Ehlers frame theory

Ehlers 1981
;
*2019_Ehlers_RepNewtLimit
(see also
Buchert and Mädler 2019
),
Natário 2006
provides a Newtonian potential to what he calls
Newtonian spacetimes
whose metric includes that of coordinate vorticity-free
R
-Warp models. Nevertheless, the proposed potential there needs careful interpretation, and cannot easily be related to
\Psi
from above.

Now, we can assess better why the meaning of
E_{\text{tot}}
is not clear, and why it is not a good measure of energy for such spacetimes:

(
i
)

Computing
E_{\text{tot}}
from (
4.1
) and using (
3.29
), we find (cf.
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Equation (7.17))
)

E_{\text{tot}}=-\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Omega^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x\leq 0\,,

(4.7)

which is different from zero for
\bm{\Omega}\neq\bm{0}
, whereas the ADM energy is always zero because of the asymptotic flatness of the
\{t=\mbox{\rm const}.\}
-slices in (
3.26
). We stress again that if one means that the ADM energy is computed with respect to another foliation, while assuming that the “standard foliation” is not asymptotically flat (but the spacetime is, which is necessary to talk about the ADM energy), this should be clearly said, thereby satisfying
I.1
.

(
ii
)

Contrary to the ADM energy which is time-independent for asymptotically flat spacetimes (cf., e.g.,
(
Gourgoulhon 2012
, Section 8.3.5)
), hence making it a reasonable notion of global energy,
E_{\text{tot}}
is not, in general; one has to use the conservation equation (
2.36
) for
E
which is equivalent to (
3.33b
).

(
iii
)

E_{\text{tot}}
depends on the foliation, whereas the ADM energy does not.

(
iv
)

Finally, as we saw in
IV.24
, it is not clear if or how
E_{\text{tot}}
is related to a Newtonian potential.

The core idea of this section is ignored in countless works in the literature.

Error 9
.

In many works,
E_{\text{tot}}
in (
4.1
) is taken to be the well-defined definition of the energy for such spacetimes, and in some cases equivalent to the ADM mass, e.g., by
Lobo and Visser 2004a
,
Santiago et al. 2022
in particular Equation (7.14) therein,
Schuster et al. 2023
, and the first version by
Clough et al. 2024
(cf.
IV.24
). To avoid the exhaustive list, we only underscore this misunderstanding in the recent works (and the references therein) by:
Alcubierre and Lobo 2017
,

Lentz 2021a

Bobrick and Martire 2021
,
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
,
Santiago et al. 2022
,
Schuster et al. 2023
,
Clough et al. 2024
.

One possible source of this confusion in the literature would be that the local definition of the rest mass
M_{\mathcal{D}}
introduced in (
2.44
) is taken to be
E_{\text{tot}}
in (
4.1
). But, these are two different concepts and may not be related to each other directly; only in special cases the former merely constitutes a part of the latter (see, e.g.,
(
Baumgarte and Shapiro 2010
, Exercise 3.23)
).

Nonetheless, if
E_{\text{tot}}
in (
4.1
) is not a meaningful one for the total mass (energy) of the warp bubble in R-Warp models, then one can ask what would constitute an appropriate (quasi-local) definition in this context when
E_{\text{ADM}}=0
?

Clough et al. 2024
give some insight into the meaning of the naive definition (
4.1
), which clearly aligns with what is mentioned above as they report that the “final mass of the spacetime volume [i.e., the naive definition of the total mass] is more positive (having started at zero).” However, a rigorous investigation of a proper definition of the energy of
R
-Warp models is beyond the scope of the present paper.

To summarize, we conclude with two important statements:
(i)
the naive definition of total mass (energy) (
4.1
) has no obvious meaning for
R
-Warp models,
(ii)

E_{\text{tot}}\neq E_{\text{ADM}}=0
for
R
-Warp models, in general.

IV.4
Symmetries and Killing vector fields

Sometimes there are some misconceptions about the symmetry of the warp-drive spacetimes, e.g., that they might be spherically symmetric (which was pointed out by
Santiago et al. 2022
). In this section, we gather other misunderstandings appearing in the warp-drive literature.

Error 10
.

Bobrick and Martire 2021
claim that both the Alcubierre and Natário metrics are spherically symmetric. This claim was correctly criticized by
Santiago et al. 2022
to which we refer the reader for further details. Then,
Bobrick and Martire 2021
state that superluminal warp drives, including the Alcubierre or Natário ones, cannot be spherically symmetric. This is again a misunderstanding since those metrics are not spherically symmetric anyway. This wrong statement is repeated by
Helmerich et al. 2024
, despite being rightly refuted in
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Appendix A)
.

Error 11
.

Bobrick and Martire 2021
use “a global Killing vector field” to classify their warp proposals. Moreover, their primary focus is put on the existence of a global timelike Killing vector field. However, this consideration is pron to some problems:

(
i
)

It is by no means clear what this Killing vector is, how it manifests itself in such spacetimes in general, and why they are needed at all.

(
ii
)

As criticized correctly by
Santiago et al. 2022
(see also the relevant critique in
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Appendix C)
), if a proposed warp model admits a global timelike Killing vector, then the corresponding warp bubble is at rest with respect to a static observer at infinity; in other words there is no motion, let alone a superluminal one, i.e., the ultimate goal of a warp drive (this misconception is repeated by
Abellán et al. 2023b
). The same mistake is repeated by
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
, where the authors assume that the shift vector potential is time-independent (see
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Appendix C)
for more critiques).

(
iii
)

They claim that the Alcubierre and even the Natário metrics do admit a global timelike Killing vector, which is clearly wrong.

(
iv
)

As an example for a possible spacelike Killing vector field, one can take the shift vector to be a Killing vector for the Euclidean metric, which results in a flat spacetime (cf.
(
Natário 2002
, Corollary 1.6)
).

(
v
)

They claim this Killing vector allows for a
3+1
decomposition without saying how exactly. All these (and more) demonstrate a misunderstanding and misuse of the notion of Killing vector fields (cf.
I.1
).

Error 12
.

As pointed out correctly by
Santiago et al. 2022
,
Bobrick and Martire 2021
were confused by a wrong perception of coordinate transformation, which was repeated uncritically by
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
.
Despite the right critique by
Santiago et al. 2022
,
Helmerich et al. 2024
made another wrong statement that the Alcubierre and Van Den Broeck metrics can be transformed to time-independent metrics, i.e., admitting global timelike Killing vector field, by transforming to what they call “constant velocity comoving frame” via a “Galilean transformation,” hence dropping some time-derivatives in the calculation (cf.
Section

IV.7
below).
Fuchs et al. 2024
make the same mistake around which they build their central concept.

IV.5
Kinematics

An in-depth treatment of kinematics of
R
-Warp models lies beyond the scope of this work.
In this section, we will summarize some mistakes regarding the kinematics in an Error.

Error 13
.

Fuchs et al. 2024
consider the
3+1
-form of geodesic equations. Apart from being formally unclear as to what, e.g., the operator
{\mathrm{d}}/{\mathrm{d}}t
means exactly (cf.
I.1
and
33
), they make several mistakes upon which they build their proposal. First, they consider the Alcubierre metric (cf.
Section

III.8
), and claim that the spatial derivative of the shift vector vanishes “inside the passenger volume region” (because the metric should be flat, cf.
33
), hence concluding from the geodesic equation that
{\mathrm{d}}u_{i}/{\mathrm{d}}t=0
, where
u_{i}
is the covariant component of the unit normal vector field (flow-orthogonal motion). However, both claims are wrong; the derivative of the shift vector does
not
vanish inside the bubble, and although the latter equation holds, the reason is not the one the authors gave, but the fact that
u_{i}=0
always holds in this case (see
III.13
).
Second, the authors take
u^{i}
and
u_{i}
to be a vector and its covector, respectively, on the spatial slices, which is wrong, and might imply
u^{i}=V^{i}={\mathrm{d}}x^{i}/{\mathrm{d}}t\equiv 0
due to the previous mistake. This furthermore shows a confusion regarding the
3
-velocity (coordinate velocity); the coordinate velocity of the physical Eulerian observers for the Alcubierre warp drive is by definition
\bm{V}=-\bm{N}
(or
{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}/{\mathrm{d}}t=-\beta^{i}
in their notation) and in this case given by (
2.15
) and not
u_{i}/u^{0}
as written by
Fuchs et al. 2024
, since the latter is identically zero, and has nothing to do with the spatial metric as they claim in and around
(
Fuchs et al. 2024
, Equation (8))
.
19
19

19

This consideration would make sense in the context of a
T
-Warp
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
, which clearly is not what the authors aimed at.
Hence, the
3+1
form of the geodesic equation does not say anything more than the very property of the flow-orthogonal motion. Hence, the whole conceptualization of a “constant velocity warp drive” by
Fuchs et al. 2024
has no meaning as long as no distinction is made between
u^{i}
and
u_{i}
.
In general, it seems that the authors have in mind a Newtonian background where the shift vector describes some motion on it (see also
33
), e.g., by writing: “[…] a warp drive that can enable the transport of different observers can do so using a shift vector inside the passenger volume,” which lacks clear meaning, especially since the shift vector is a choice of coordinate system.
Even if it was clear what
Fuchs et al. 2024
mean by this, their model suffers from other serious problems, see, e.g.
18
and
Appendix

B
.

IV.6
The Synge G-method and unphysical solutions

In general, there are several ways one can approach GR. In particular, for solving the Einstein field equations there are two different ways: from “right to left,” or “backwards” (see
Ellis and Garfinkle 2024
;
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
).
However,
Synge 1971
identifies three distinct “spirits” of investigation regarding the Einstein field equations: the
realistic
, who seeks to align the equations with their “already extensive knowledge of the physical universe” (from “right to left”); the
agonistic
, who focuses on mastering the inherent mathematical difficulties of the field equations; and the
creative
, whose “pleasure” lies in the “construction of universes,
fantastic
or
realistic
, satisfying the field equations” (from “left to right”). This creative approach relies on what is known as the G-method (cf.
Ellis and Garfinkle 2024
;
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
), a process where the metric tensor is given (hence, the left side of the Einstein equation) to mathematically determine the energy-momentum tensor (the right side). While this “backwards” methodology allows for the generation of exact solutions through differentiation rather than solving complex partial differential equations, its unrestricted use often leads to “fantastic” results that may not correspond to the physical spacetime, potentially violating not only the energy conditions but also some stability conditions (see
Ellis and Garfinkle 2024
).

The creative Synge G-method is the basis of almost all works in the warp-drive literature, with a few exceptions (e.g.,
Low 1999
;
Clough et al. 2024
) that represent a correct initial value formulation (IVF) (despite Errors
9
and
35
). The ambiguity in taking a clear approach is often misunderstood, overlooked, or ignored, which led to some Errors that we mention in the following.

Remark IV.25
.

The approach of the present work is to some extent agonistic; indeed, prior to attach meaning to a solution, even prior to solving Einstein’s equations (and often merely trying to respect
I.1
), we managed to identify the misconceptions and misunderstandings embracing the current proposals for warp drive. Although, all three “spirits” recognized by
Synge 1971
are essential to understand the Einstein field equations better, we clearly advocate more the realistic and agonistic approaches, rather than the creative one.

Remark IV.26
.

To some extent, there is a fundamental difference between two FTL proposals, i.e., the wormhole and the warp drive, in that the former can exist eternally, whereas the latter is meant to be built. Therefore, an IVF for the latter is essential while the former does not necessarily need it.

Error 14
.

Bobrick and Martire 2021
claim that the continuity equations are violated for the Alcubierre warp drive. This statement is manifestly wrong, and is based on a naive Newtonian viewpoint, as pointed out correctly by
Santiago et al. 2022
, since by the Synge-G method, any given (regular enough) Lorentzian metric will, by the field equations, produce an energy-momentum tensor that satisfies the continuity equation. This error likely influenced
Lentz 2021b
, as it was absent in the original paper
Lentz 2021a
. Moreover,
Bobrick and Martire 2021
attributes this fact to asymptotically flat spacetimes which is again wrong. This mistake was corrected by
Helmerich et al. 2024
without referring to
Santiago et al. 2022
which already had pointed at this mistake. Furthermore,
Helmerich et al. 2024
reframe this fact as a requirement which is not correct since any diffeomorphism invariant theory enforces the energy-momentum tensor to be divergence free (cf., e.g.,
(
Wald 1984
, Appendix E)
).

Error 15
.

The misunderstanding and misconception explained in
14
, led to another publication by
Carneiro et al. 2022b
, which is entirely based on the wrong statement with the hope to solve this pseudo-problem (cf.
I.1
) by going beyond GR.

Error 16
.

Lentz 2021a
proposes a “hyperbolic shift vector” which is sourced by a scalar field that satisfies some “spatial” hyperbolic equation with a source, i.e., there is no time involved, and the “propagation” occurs not in time, but in one spatial direction (see also
17
). Then, the author argues that such a construction can be done utilizing plasma matter. It is by no means clear why plasma should be a candidate as the author does not give support as to what phenomena lead to an equation of motion similar to the hyperbolic one given in
Lentz 2021a
. To our knowledge, a somewhat similar equation of motion shows up in the context of the supersonic flow (cf., e.g.,
Tempest 1949
) which clearly was not the motivation for
Lentz 2021a
. In any case, as pointed out in
(
Santiago et al. 2022
, Appendix C)
, the proposal (“hyperbolic-shift-vector-plasma construction”) by
Lentz 2021a
is not a solution to Einstein’s equations, hence violating even the Synge G-method (see also
Section

IV.10
).
20
20

20

There are even more ambiguities in
Lentz 2021a
. Looking at
(
Lentz 2021a
, Equations (26)–(29))
, it is not clear if the
4
-velocity
u^{\mu}
therein is aligned with the unit normal vector (see
R1
), suggesting that the model might be what we refer to as a
T
-Warp model (see
Barzegar and Buchert 2025
), which is certainly not what the author intended to.

Error 17
.

Lentz 2021a
claims that
\bm{J}=\bm{0}
because of the particular choice of the shift vector (the “hyperbolic shift vector”). This is, however, related to the form of the shift vector in (
3.36
), not the property of the potential function considered there, and it follows directly by the Synge G-method.

Obviously, independent of the approach, statements regarding the given spacetime should be about solutions to Einstein’s equation. In
16
we pointed at one instance where this was not the case. In the following we identify another appearance of a similar error.

Error 18
.

The construction proposed by
Fuchs et al. 2024
is not a solution to Einstein’s equations; indeed, as we showed in
Appendix

B
, the authors do not solve the Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff (TOV) equations resulting from the Einstein equation in the given context. Hence, the algorithm in
Fuchs et al. 2024
for “metric creation” is flawed. This is yet another example of violation of
I.1
.

IV.7
Numerics and simulations

There are some numerical works in the literature regarding warp-drive spacetimes. As before, we put an emphasis on the recent works which claim to propose a physical warp drive.

Based on the discussion in
Section

IV.6
, the only clear and sound work in the warp-drive literature, to our knowledge, is the work by
Clough et al. 2024
, in which the authors correctly show that if one starts with the Alcubierre metric as initial data (and with a stiff fluid), one ends up with instability. This can be seen even with a
quasi-Newtonian
approach, since on the spatial slices everything reduce to vector analysis where the collapse is readily seen. And all this happens already for the subluminal case to which they restricted themselves. Nonetheless, this work exhibits some shortcomings, including Errors
9
,
35
.

Error 19
.

Helmerich et al. 2024
(among others) claim (which likely is motivated by
Lentz 2021a
,
(
Lentz 2021b
, Section 5)
,
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
, and perhaps
Abellán et al. 2024
): “Warp drive research has been mostly derived by analytic studies of various metrics. Such limitations have led to a narrow perspective when it comes to warp drive physicality, especially due to the non-linear nature of the gravitational field equations.” This implies that the fundamental mistakes, misconceptions, misunderstandings (at some of which
Santiago et al. 2022
and the present work pointed), and even more the lack of a clear proposal for a warp drive (cf.
Section

IV.10
below), can be avoided if one exploits numerical analysis. This view can only lead to another pseudo-problem (cf.
I.1
), since if a model has formal (and fundamental) problems, one cannot circumvent those by numerics or simulations. They further claim: “The key challenge in warp research is two-fold: The first is evaluating the complicated Einstein field equations to find stress-energy tensors and the second is in evaluating those stress-energy tensors for physicality,” which, beside being not the primary issue of the “warp research,” shows the unfamiliarity of the authors with the numerical relativity (see
Clough et al. 2024
for a relevant example, cf., also,
Section

IV.6
). Moreover,
Helmerich et al. 2024
, claim: “Traditionally, the analysis of physicality in these metrics has been limited to considering the energy density observed by Eulerian observers. However, this approach alone is insufficient for a thorough evaluation of the energy conditions. To overcome these limitations and enable the exploration of more complex solutions, Warp Factory employs a numerical framework,” which is again not true since it was mostly the “tradition” of the authors, as for example this point (that was, among others, claimed by
Bobrick and Martire 2021
) was correctly criticized by
Santiago et al. 2022
without using numerics.

Error 20
.

Some works, including the ones by
Abellán et al. 2024
,
Helmerich et al. 2024
, and to a great extent
Fuchs et al. 2024
, do not perform a proper
3+1
-numerics as it should be done, i.e., they suffer from an unclear IVF (see the only correct work by
Clough et al. 2024
in this field of research), which caused various errors and ambiguities (cf. also
13
).

Remark IV.27
.

In general, it seems that
Fuchs et al. 2024
;
Helmerich et al. 2024
;
Lentz 2021a
;
Fell and Heisenberg 2021
;
Abellán et al. 2024
;
Bolívar et al. 2025
rushed to plot some figures without an in-depth analysis, thereby making fundamental mistakes.

We finish this part by mentioning that the system of equations in
Section

III.6
in terms of principal invariants may turn out to be useful for further numerical experiments.

IV.8
Energy conditions and their consequences

As mentioned in
Section

I
, the main focus of the majority of the works on warp-drive spacetimes has been put on the validity or violation of energy conditions, without tackling all the other pathologies we mentioned in this work. This is why we bring this section quite in the end.

We try to gather the results on the energy conditions for
R
-Warp models.
Although, it is somewhat easy to show the violation of the NEC, hence all the other energy conditions (see
Figure

1
), for the Alcubierre metric (see, e.g.,
Santiago et al. 2022
), the corresponding task is not as easy for a more general model.
For
R
-Warp models, a proof was finally given by
Santiago et al. 2022
applying
III.1
and
R4
properly.

Moreover, using (
3.29
) and (
4.1
),
Santiago et al. 2022
show that
R
-Warp models with
\bm{\Omega}\neq\bm{0}
do violate the WEC (see, however,
29
for a mistake which does not change this result). But, while
Shoshany and Snodgrass 2024
showed that asymptotically flat warp drives with a generic lapse function, introduced in
Section

III.4
, with
\bm{\Omega}\neq\bm{0}
indeed violate the WEC, they demonstrated that one cannot conclude the violation of the WEC when
\bm{\Omega}=\bm{0}
, in general. This, of course, applies for the unit lapse case, i.e.,
R
-Warp models.

This is an interesting case; indeed, since we know that the NEC is violated by
Theorem

IV.34
below, the WEC is also violated. But, this example shows that sometimes it is easier to confer the NEC than the WEC, although the latter is weaker than the former.

On the other hand, in many works it is suggested that the superluminality is a reason for the violation of (some) energy conditions. This is however not true, in general, and the violation of energy conditions occurs way before considering the speed of the warp bubble (see, e.g.,
(
Lobo and Visser 2004a
, Section 4)
).

We give a definition for the pointwise
21
21

21

Or “pointilliste,” contrasted with the averaged energy conditions, or “impressionist” one; both introduced by
Curiel 2017
, borrowing terminology from the modern art movement. The former implies the latter, but not vice versa.
energy conditions in the following and refer the reader to, e.g.,
(
Hawking and Ellis 2023
, Section 4)
, and especially
Curiel 2017
for an in-depth treatment of this topic (see also
Kontou and Sanders 2020
for a recent account).

The pointwise (classical) energy conditions are, according to the hierarchy (cf.
Figure

1
):

(
i
)

The null energy condition

T_{\mu\nu}k^{\mu}k^{\nu}\geq 0\,;\,\,\text{for all null vectors}\,\,\bm{k}=k^{\mu}\bm{\partial}_{\mu}\,,

(NEC)

with its geometric form
\textbf{Ric}(\bm{k},\bm{k})\geq 0
, by the Einstein equation.

(
ii
)

The weak energy condition

T_{\mu\nu}t^{\mu}t^{\nu}\geq 0\,;\,\,\text{for all timelike vectors}\,\,\bm{t}=t^{\mu}\bm{\partial}_{\mu}\,,

(WEC)

with its geometric form
\bm{G}(\bm{k},\bm{k})\geq 0
, where
\bm{G}
is the Einstein tensor (
2.49
).

(
iii
)

The dominant energy condition

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}t^{\mu}t^{\nu}\geq 0\,;

\displaystyle\text{for all timelike vectors}\,\,\bm{t}\,,

(DEC)

\displaystyle\text{such that}\,\,\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}t^{\nu}\,\,\text{is causal},

with its geometric equivalent statement by replacing
\bm{T}
above by
\bm{G}
.
22
22

22

See, e.g.,
Curiel 2017
for an alternative formulation.

(
iv
)

The strong energy condition

\displaystyle(T_{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}T_{{\mu\nu}})t^{\mu}t^{\nu}\geq 0\,;

\displaystyle\text{for all timelike}

(SEC)

\displaystyle\text{ vectors}\,\,\bm{t}\,,

with its geometric form
\textbf{Ric}(\bm{t},\bm{t})\geq 0
, by the Einstein equation.

Remark IV.28
.

On top of the physical and geometric definitions of the energy conditions, there is an
effective
definition which is based on the quantitative relations between energy density
\hat{\epsilon}
and principal pressures
\hat{p}_{\hat{\mu}}
in an orthonormal frame where the energy-momentum tensor takes special forms, most importantly, if it is of Hawking–Ellis type I, meaning the energy-momentum tensor is diagonal with respect to the orthonormal frame (see, e.g.,
Hawking and Ellis 2023
;
Curiel 2017
;
Santiago et al. 2022
for more details). In this special case, we have

(
i
)

the NEC is equivalent to

\hat{\epsilon}+\hat{p}_{\hat{\mu}}\geq 0\,;\,\,\forall\,\hat{\mu}\in\{1,2,3\}\,,

(
ii
)

the WEC is equivalent to
\hat{\epsilon}\geq 0
and

\hat{\epsilon}+\hat{p}_{\hat{\mu}}\geq 0\,;\,\,\forall\,\hat{\mu}\in\{1,2,3\}\,,

(
iii
)

the DEC is equivalent to
\hat{\epsilon}\geq 0
and

\hat{\epsilon}\geq|\hat{p}_{\hat{\mu}}|\,;\,\,\forall\,\hat{\mu}\in\{1,2,3\}\,,

(
iv
)

and the SEC is equivalent to
\hat{\epsilon}+\sum_{\hat{\mu}}\hat{p}_{\hat{\mu}}\geq 0
and

\hat{\epsilon}+\hat{p}_{\hat{\mu}}\geq 0\,;\,\,\forall\,\hat{\mu}\in\{1,2,3\}\,.

Before continuing to the next section, we will summarize some general misunderstandings and mistakes in the following.

Error 21
.

In many works,
23
23

23

See also
Footnote

26
.
some fundamental misunderstandings about the notion of energy conditions, in general, and the WEC, in particular, can be found (cf.
28
). For example, in a recent work by
Abellán et al. 2024
one reads: “Energy conditions are expressions that can be used to relate the quantities that appear in the energy-momentum tensor
T_{\mu\nu}
. As is well known, these expressions are used more as a rough guideline than as a constraint on these quantities,” which evidently points at one of these misunderstandings (see also
22
), and another example of violation of
I.1
.

Error 22
.

Abellán et al. 2024
make a mistake when deriving the equivalent inequalities to the energy conditions. Those inequalities do not hold as they are written. We do not want to analyze them, since the work suffers from a bigger error, i.e.,
23
. However, we highlight the inconsistency with the subsequent work by the same authors for the case of vanishing “heat flow”
Bolívar et al. 2025
.

:
